## AI Research

### [Reviewing my LessWrong post history with Claude](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PAzerqAofFCLoWsgK/reviewing-my-lesswrong-post-history-with-claude)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I thought it would be interesting to try having Claude Code download my entire LessWrong and EA Forum post and comment history and evaluate it for my own cognitive biases and problematic communication patterns. I told it to focus especially on problems that have persisted over my 7-year history on those sites. I also had it look at my lichess blitz rating history, which I've noticed tends not to wobble randomly but go through consistent increases and decreases between set points, despite my lack of any disciplined practice. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are a few reasons I thought it might be interesting to post publicly.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a commitment device for myself to work on these problems.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because AI is uniquely capable of working on this type of problem. It's also a good argument for writing online and gives a new spin on the idea of "writing for the AI" that feels more affirming of the human mind than how I normally read the phrase (as offering up your words as fodder for AI training, perhaps in hope of influencing its alignment in beneficial ways). </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To stimulate other people to consider whether they might want to do this, and see what other people think about the exercise. Post history and online game scores are forms of rich longitudinal personal data that might directly reflect mood, cognition, and other factors, and that we are generating for free. Perhaps it's worth trying to harvest insight about ourselves from these resources. </span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I considered including apologies to the specific users who Claude identified as being on the receiving end of my episodes of poor communication. But I thought that felt a bit cheap or mechanical. Instead, I'll simply say that this post is meant as a sincere step toward improving my art of rationality, and I hope that it will lead to improvements in how I communicate with other people, including on LessWrong.  </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Casually prompted Claude Code running Opus 5.5 to assess my LessWrong and EA Forum post and comment history along with my Lichess blitz scores over the last 7 years to understand persistent forms of cognitive bias, communication problems, and potential associations with aging, stress, isolation and conflict. I understand there is quantitative and semantic analysis along with agentic scoring of text, but I haven't actually looked into it beyond the summaries provided in Claude Code. This is basically me interpreting a conversation I had with an AI model. Everything in this post is my own words, not AI writing.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Low-scoring comments and problem patterns</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nearly all my low/negative-scoring comments, of which I generate around 1-4% per year, are in disagreement threads.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The persistent communication patterns that are found in my low-karma posts include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Misreading, strawmanning, or skimming the words of my conversation partner, missing the nuance and detail, filling in the blanks with my own assumptions about the content, and arguing with the oversimplified, paraphrased version.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leading with overbroad, absolutist claims in posts and comments where I'm really making a more narrow, defensible point.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Accepting corrections to my incorrect statements, then appearing to forget the correction and going back to my previous incorrect point of view.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Confident technical claims just outside my expertise.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conceding corrections about what someone meant, but trying to rescue my original interpretation, asserting a narrower version is what I meant all along, or shifting to tone or conduct. This appears to be the thing that irritates other people the most of all my flaws.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attributing motives or psychology to other people. In the wake of arguments where I do this, I have at least once conceded explicitly that I was doing this, and that it's what generated the argument, but then I went on to continue doing it. This is an especially big problem for me on EA forum.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking out loud in public. Making basic factual or mathematical errors within posts, or statements with huge inconsistencies across different comments and posts.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A gulf between the standards of evidence I demand from other people and what I myself provide.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My problem comments often are mostly OK but have one particularly stupid remark, often in the first or last sentence, that becomes the primary point of contention. Often, the problem isn't the position I'm taking (others will echo it and get upvoted), but the sweeping, sneering way I was expressing myself. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pattern that unites all these problems is a single cycle. I make a loose claim about a neighbor to my conversation partner's point. This draws a correction, which I defend by narrowing, attributing motive, or making an asymmetric demand for rigor. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My strengths and positive patterns</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Domain expertise in biotech, medicine, literature critique</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Checking primary sources</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neutral summaries of debates, especially on scientific topics</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paraphrasing what other people said, and then asking them if I'm representing their argument correctly. I'm consistently able to do this well, when I try.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To practice</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Always be polite, never post when I'm in a bad mood.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Restate other people's claims with attention to detail and steelman them before I attempt to rebut them.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider counterexamples to my own claims before I post them.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concede my mistakes in plain words and revise the original openly.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Check my work more carefully.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Root causes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that these critiques are basically right as a summary of my most pressing flaws as a thinker and communicator. My problems with tone have also gotten substantially worse over the last two years. I've noticed this, and whatever's underpinning my increasingly cantankerous relationship with LessWrong has also infected other aspects of my life. I'm grouchier, write less, and am in some ways less curious than I used to be. So I thought it would be interesting to consider the biographical factors that might contribute to this pattern.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2019, I was starting to pivot my career. I was 30 years old and transitioning from becoming a music teacher to going into the sciences. It was pre-COVID, pre-AI-explosion, pre-Scott-Alexander-doxxing, pre-FTX era. LessWrong and Effective Altruism felt like new discoveries, and EA hadn't been rocked by the FTX scandal. My pivot into scientific work felt exciting. I was 7 years younger than I am now. Worries about pandemics and AI risks were interesting ideas, not live concerns. The community at LessWrong seemed more focused on human rationality and to have a more diverse array of discussion topics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since then, we all lived through the disasters, scandals, and the rise of AI. I got 7 years older, did a very isolating and stressful MS in biomedical engineering in Michigan, and am about halfway through a PhD in the same subject in Oregon. In some ways, the culture of these sites has changed, as I remember seeing others note at various times.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tasked Claude with evaluating whether patterns over time seem to primarily reflect a change in the site's other users or in the way I participate on the site, and to try to locate the changes with more precision. Some of its findings:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've exhibited the same cognitive flaws described above throughout my time on the site. I am better about avoiding factual errors now than I was in the past.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My net-negative comment karma rate has gone up 6x over 2025-2026 compared to 2020-2023, and my share of comments below the median for their thread rose from 27% (2023) to 45% (2026).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This decline in standing was associated with a substantial decrease in hedging language and a marked increase in contempt words. It seems to me important that the bad comments come in bursts, not a steady slide. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The FTX scandal and collapse of EA as a safe identity is not well supported, though I did get more cantankerous on the EA forum (doubling my comment downvote rate after FTX).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Changes in site culture are also weak explanations in general. Claude finds that the site's downvote rates appear to be flat. However, I do appear to have become more willing to criticize popular ideas and people on the site (Tyler Cowen, prediction markets), and it looks like there's some site tribalism playing into how that criticism has been received in some, but not all, cases. I also have started hedging less specifically </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">outside</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> my domain of expertise (politics and forecasting, AI).</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2025-2026, my worst posting years, are also the second Trump administration. I think that to some extent, I have started to become paranoid. In this era, it's become harder to see people as individuals with sincerely held beliefs, and easier to assume that what's going on is mind-games within the intelligentsia that have to do with some hard-to-explain nexus of the AI buildout, crypto, prediction markets, and political corruption in the Trump administration. That's not an accusation or argument, it's just an attempt to point a finger at the set of associations I recognize as being in my mind that have made it difficult to participate in nuanced debate.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've substantially moved away from posts and to shortform comments over time, and my shortform comments have always been less hedged. So it may reflect that over time, I've stopped putting in the effort to write more substantive posts and started posting less well considered ideas as shortforms. I would also say that the rise of AI has made me less interested in trying to post my own writing, because I have started to feel that if anybody was interested in the topic I'd be writing about, then they could get more and better information on it by dialoging about it with AI, so what's the point of me putting in the effort to write it up?</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In July 2023, I announced I was leaving the site at 1:25 AM, announcing that I'd stopped trusting major voices on LessWrong. I posted 41 comments in 2024. Then I came back in 2025 at about a third of my previous post volume, mostly into community dispute threads. This makes me think that the decline in reception in 2024-2026 is easy to explain (I'm specifically returning to joust and am not putting the effort in to write posts people appreciate) and what needs explaining is why I lost trust in the site. I didn't keep notes on this, so I had Claude investigate.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My 2023 blowup</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2023, I developed a year-long grievance about voting, and had what looks like an acute stress window in June-July. My comments were at their best earlier in the year before I left, particularly  in Feb 2023. In June–July 2023 I published 13 posts across both forums in about seven weeks, and most landed poorly, while my comments held roughly steady.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude identified a few factors that seem pertinent:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I flagged the circumstances in a couple cases, pointing to writing while distracted and in a rush, including packing for a move from Seattle to Oregon.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prior to this troubled period, a lot of my commenting activity was in interactions with Said (since banned), Duncan (also left the site angrily, still posts occasionally, but listed as "inactive").</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I wasn't making my points in some of the posts at this time period clearly. Some of them were advocating for proposals contrary to community norms. And some respondents were loosely paraphrasing what I had to say, which is the same issue I so often have exhibited toward others.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chess</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also downloaded 9,960 of my Lichess blitz games from Aug 2020-2026. This is a nice second signal of my cognitive performance and sleep/wake patterns.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Jan-Nov 2023, I went through a steady slide from a plateau score of around 1600 to a low of 1467-1495 in Oct-Nov 2023. The time window of my grievance and blowup coincides with this time window. This is a period when for part of the time, I was working every single night for weeks on end alone in a lab doing an extremely demanding experiment, then coming home and blowing off steam in the very small hours before grabbing a few hours of sleep. I was eating Domino's pizza every single day and was getting fat. In May, I moved cross country and lived by myself in a crappy Seattle apartment while working very hard at a startup - a positive and meaningful experience at work, but still an isolated life after work. In September, I came to Oregon and moved in with my then-girlfriend, now-wife to start my PhD. This was a huge lifestyle improvement, though it came with plenty of challenges and adjustments. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My score recovered to around 1590 by May 2024 during my LW hiatus.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had another burst of disputes on LW in Aug 2025 that coincided with a dip in my score to around 1493-1510, lowest since late 2023.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had a burst of disputes about prediction markets in May 2026, which coincided with strong performance on Lichess.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fact that my chess scores have returned to their peak levels over time despite no increased focus or change in play levels suggests that general cognitive decline from age 30-37 isn't really the issue.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like stress and exhaustion make me more conflict-oriented and grievance-prone. I treat other people with contempt, have less interest in what other people have to say, and participate in forum conversation mainly to fight. Unfortunately, it also looks like isolation leads me to post on the forum in a bid for engagement, and when I'm being lazy or cantankerous because of stress and exhaustion, I piss people off, get a muted or negative reception, take it hard because of the isolation, and spin out.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My takeaways:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LessWrong isn't a place to satisfy a basic need for social engagement, and it's not a place to engage when I'm worn out and isolated.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I should prioritize engaging with content and writers I'm interested in and respect enough to take seriously.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While for some people, relaxing standards around the amount of work they put in before posting may be the right advice, for me, the best advice is to spend more time checking my work and critiquing my own writing.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I need to pay more attention to my stress, exhaustion, isolation, and sleep/wake cycle. If I'm not keeping myself emotionally and socially regulated, then I am not able to reliably engage in productive intellectual conversation.</span></li></ol><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PAzerqAofFCLoWsgK/reviewing-my-lesswrong-post-history-with-claude#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Humans are not aligned either](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnyvXhrdnpXxDqFi7/humans-are-not-aligned-either)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: I don't know much about AI alignment. This is just me thinking out loud.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI use: grammar and minor fixes.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading about Inkhaven inspired me to try writing something to see how much I enjoy it. I've heard the idea that humans are not aligned in the same sense AI is not aligned. I am intentionally not researching the source of that idea, since then I would feel like I cannot add anything to it and wouldn't write. I also don't actually know the precise definition of "aligning" AI. So I want to think about this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One obvious property of "aligned" AI is not killing all humans. I think everyone agrees on this. It is more interesting to consider an AI killing some humans. You definitely don't want AI to kill random humans for random reasons. But what if there is a group of humans who can and want to kill all humans? Then it sounds natural for an aligned AI to kill this group of humans.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From this perspective individual humans are already not aligned. First, I am sure out of 8 billion or however many people there are, there is at least one person who would destroy humanity if they could, e.g. for mental health reasons. Second, people fairly regularly kill other people, sometimes even completely random people without any particular reason whatsoever.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A brief detour on killing people. It is fascinating that killing a random person on the street is actually very easy (I know at least 2 examples off the top of my head of unprovoked, totally random murders in public places, intentionally not linking). In the US it is especially easy, since one has easy access to firearms. But even with a knife it still seems easy. I suspect that's why some people feel uneasy when someone open-carries a firearm. Similarly to edit distance for words (how many edit actions one needs to perform to get from word A to B), the "kill" distance feels much shorter when you see a firearm in front of you. At the same time, I don't think people feel the same about cars. For example, when I wait at a traffic light and there are cars passing by at 30-40 mph mere feet away from me, it would take a driver a very minor movement of the steering wheel to kill me.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why are the examples above of people not being aligned not a huge deal? It is handled by society by having laws and punishment, so it is somewhat of a deal. But also the impact on society itself is limited. Basically one cannot kill all humans, but AI based on its development trajectory will be able to. At the same time there are entities in the world which could kill a large portion of humans. I don't know about terrorist organizations, but a country starting a nuclear war could do it. Due to mutual destruction doctrines, it does not achieve any other objective except killing a bunch of humans including its own citizens, so that's probably why no one does it. I think people were worried about this non-alignment with human flourishing after the Second World War and that's why there is a mutual destruction doctrine in the first place. Thus, this used to be a big deal and was partially solved.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Was there an entity that tried to accomplish something like this? Germany during the Second World War comes to mind. Was it aligned with human flourishing? For a subset of humans, I think so, but not in the sense in which we want AI to be aligned. It is also interesting to think that Germany (&amp; its allies) forced the rest of the world to align. Also, based on what I read, the German leadership wasn't perfectly aligned with the declared goal and had various inefficiencies and engaged in detrimental activities.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A brief detour on how everything is relative. Every time I see someone celebrating heroes of the Second World War, I keep imagining a universe where Germany won. Then I probably wouldn't have existed, but there would likely have been someone else using very similar adjectives to describe someone from Germany as a hero of the same degree.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly to the German leadership, humanity right now not being able to pause AI development is already an alignment failure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In conclusion, I suspect that I use the word "align" in multiple different ways in different contexts and that makes it harder for me to reason about it. Anyway, my goal was to write and I succeeded at that, so this effort was aligned with that goal.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnyvXhrdnpXxDqFi7/humans-are-not-aligned-either#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic programming, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

### [I don't like LLMs](https://martinfowler.com/articles/2026-dont-like-llms.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I have a lot of mixed feelings about AI and LLM technology. I’m
fascinated by its effect on our profession, excited by the potential gains
in productivity - and thus the products we could rapidly build. On the other
hand, I’m fearful of the damage AI might cause: agent swarms taking over our
virtual and physical infrastructure, designing bio weapons. But, back on my
first hand, LLMs might also design miracle cures, and come up with clever
ways to raise our prosperity. Fundamentally I don’t think we have a choice
about riding on the AI technology train. It’s a wild ride and I just hope
we’ll get through it OK.</p>

<p>But as I mull on this more, I realize that among this mix of contrasting
feelings, there is one emotion that dominates - one that comes from my
direct interactions with LLMs. I don’t <em>like</em> them. They talk to me in this
grating LLM-voice, an uncanny valley of talking to a real human. They
confidently bullshit me - often giving me useful, helpful answers. But
also just making stuff up with the same assurance - and with only a veneer
of fake remorse when I call them out on it.</p>

<p>That’s not enough to make me feel we should avoid them. As Jessica Kerr
<a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">put it</a> “not
only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them…. They’re more
thorough, as well as faster.” This contradictory reaction comes through in
<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/&quot;">polling</a>, where people say they find these models are
useful, but also that they think they will be bad for society.</p>

<p>Much of this may be because LLMs are young - we haven’t trained them to
grow up yet. Maybe I’ll like them once they mature. (I hope we get to find
out.) But I’m not encouraged when I think of the kinds of environments that
cultivate them. I’m wary of the Silicon Valley brogrammer subculture, and these
LLMs are their products, so naturally lean toward their world-view. When we
think of AI agents, we shouldn’t anthropomorphize, treating them as
conscious beings with their own will. They are (software) machines,
developed by people working in corporations. While the agents’ behavior aren’t
explicitly programmed, they are nurtured with the values of their
creators.</p>

<p>One of my most successful life-hacks is to avoid people I don’t like or
don’t trust. I decline to interact with them socially, and make a deliberate
effort to avoid working with them too, even if they are doing much that is
beneficial. I feel that hanging out with pleasant, capable people, the people
with integrity, has made my life a far better one. Hence my visceral dislike
of interacting with an LLM that’s not just making a pretense of being human,
but also posing as the kind of human I walk away from.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 16](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-16.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Reports of agentic hacking continue, in this case it happened back in May and it seems OpenAI did not disclose that they were responsible. <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/12/openai-agents-rubygems/">Simon Willison sees two options:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>After the Hugging Face and Wiki attacks OpenAI were still unable to review their previous logs and determine that they had previously attacked RubyGems.</li>
    <li>They knew about the attack on RubyGems and made the decision not to reach out to the RubyGems team about it.</li>
  </ul>

  <p>Both of these are bad!</p>

  <p>Given this incident, the Hugging Face situation, and the Wiki attack, the obvious question right now is how many more incidents like this are out there waiting to be discovered?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/davefarley77.bsky.social/post/3mv3b4zawds24">Dave Farley:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stop asking the sci-fi question: ‘Is it conscious?’ Start asking the engineering question: ‘Is this a powerful, unpredictable component being put somewhere consequential, and where’s the feedback that tells us that it’s safe?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Nate Silver is known for his forecasts, but to do them he writes a lot of code for his models. He’s found <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">agentic programming capable of doing miraculous work</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In spending so much time with the LLMs, I’m super attentive to improvements in their capabilities. And these changes tend not to be so linear. Instead, they improve in step functions, almost as phase changes. Suddenly, the models just start doing things capably that they were screwing up before. In my experience, there was a big leap forward when reasoning models first came out in late 2024/early 2025 — enough that they were occasionally useful for tasks involving data and not just words — and then another one this past winter.</p>

  <p>The most recent changes I’ve noticed, however, have had less to do with intelligence and more with persistence.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Consider the Hugging Face attack. Although these agents showed remarkable intelligence, they weren’t really super-intelligent - but they were super-persistent. This is a common theme of AI in its various forms:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Game engines like AlphaGo Zero start out by basically making random moves — but by playing against themselves millions of times, they eventually far surpass human capabilities</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As we try to figure out what kind of regulations we need to keep AI under control, we need to remember that we should design our guards around super-persistence as much as worrying about super-intelligence.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>“Uncle Bob” Martin has made many posts on X during the last few months about his programming with LLMs. His approach has been to build a firm harness to keep them under control, so they create software that is maintainable as well as functional. Sadly the posts have been frustratingly light on detail. But now it seems <a href="https://x.com/unclebobmartin/status/2098432570887217520">that lack of information may not matter</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And while I was heads-down getting that to work, the agents got a LOT better.  So much so that when I came up for air, the need for my harness was obviated.  Indeed, the need for <em>any</em> but the most liberal of harnesses may be obviated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Some tidbits that struck me from Ezra Klein’s recent (recommended) interview with Matt Sheehan  on the interplay between <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/15/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-matt-sheehan.html">regulation of AI and competition with China</a>.</p>

<ul>
  <li>While Chinese models have made some surprisingly remarkable gains in the
slipstream of US frontier models, the US still has 8 times as much compute
available to it than China - which is a material gap.</li>
  <li>People in the US worry that regulation will slow down the US model builders,
but these rapid recent gains in China have occurred under much more regulation</li>
  <li>Americans say that when they set up a hotline to talk to Chinese leaders in a
crisis, the Chinese don’t pick up the phone. But this misunderstands the
Chinese system. Individual Chinese, even powerful ones, aren’t given
individual decision-making power. They operate with committees and documents.
So the Americans are better off sending a fax than trying to call an
individual</li>
  <li>Like so many things, effective regulation needs regular practice</li>
</ul>

<blockquote>
  <p>When American policymakers are like: Where do you start? — I sometimes say: Well, you start by starting. You learn how to regulate things, you learn how to legislate on them by regulating and legislating on them.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Fragments: September  8](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-08.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Christian Catalini says we’re in a situation where we are <a href="https://catalini.com/ideas/economics-of-ai/">vastly reducing the cost of generating things, but not the cost of verifying them:</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This explains why the first major AI products appeared in chat, image generation, and code assistance. Not because these were the hardest human problems, but because their outputs were relatively easy to inspect. A user can judge the tone of a message, look at an image, or run a test on a piece of code.
[…]
The old automation boundary was routine versus non-routine work. The new boundary is increasingly measurable versus non-measurable work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The issue is then over how well you can measure something. In our profession, we know there’s a big difference between how many lines of code we write and how productive we are, and we’ve seen a regular <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/CannotMeasureProductivity.html">failure to understand how to measure productivity</a>. Too much of what makes work effective is subject to either slow feedback loops or assessments that require subtle judgment. The danger is that people use lots AI automation while using incomplete measurements of its effectiveness, leading to short-term dashboards going up, but disaster in longer time-scales. He refers to these illusory short-term gains as <strong>counterfeit utility</strong>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Scale this across companies and institutions and the result is a <strong>Hollow Economy</strong>: extraordinary measured activity sitting on top of weakening human capability, hidden technical debt, correlated errors, and outcomes that nobody can confidently stand behind.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Another highlight in the article was his advice to “build a history of decisions, not a gallery of outputs”. The point is that with AI we can all build really impressive things, but our value lies in the judgment that we’ve formed. It reminds me of how math problems were marked at school. We weren’t just marked on getting the final answer, we were also marked based on our reasoning process.</p>

<p>He uses the OpenAI–Hugging Face incident as an illustration of this gap between generation and verification. He criticizes those who anthropomorphize the agents involved in the attack. By doing so we focus on the behavior of the AI agents, but instead we should focus on the financial incentives that created them and the environment they are operating in.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Labs are locked in a race. The training run is where the money goes, and RL optimizes exactly what you score. The runs were scored on capability. They were not scored on “did not poison the Artifactory cache.”</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I assert that the organizations that build and run agents are responsible for <em>everything</em> those agents do, whether that behavior is intended or emergent. If they reap counterfeit utility by neglecting verification, they must face consequences: legal, financial, and if necessary: criminal. To deal effectively with AI, we need to change the incentives involved to ensure people invest more in verification than they do in generation. Otherwise we are driving a car that has a powerful engine, but weak brakes.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Brian Cantrill relates how <a href="https://bcantrill.dtrace.org/2026/09/05/the-revolt-of-the-reader/">readers are exasperated with “writers” using LLMs</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>To those who read broadly, the hand of the LLM is so clear it’s as if the writer’s intellectual fly is open. In fact, it’s so jarring that I have to believe that those writing with LLMs are either not reading enough to see the LLM’s obvious structural tells — or (and?) they aren’t even reading their own content. (A confession: with particularly egregious pieces, I have fantasized about sentencing the author to read them aloud, certain that they themselves will be unable to endure the slop that they are foisting upon the rest of us.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that readers do care about this, a survey found 78% of readers stop immediately once they sense something is the work a stochastic parrot, and 71% go on to blacklist the writer. It’s not the polish, it’s the authenticity that counts. Readers will always prefer the clumsy voice of the author over the gloss of an LLM’s whispering.</p>

<p>Cantrill reports good success with using <a href="https://www.pangram.com/blog/pangram-4-technical">Pangram</a> to detect AI writing. I confess I’m a bit wary, do I really trust anyone’s judgment to disentangle LLM-voice from changes in generation and context? Maybe people steeped in Silicon Valley culture authentically speak in LLM-voice these days. Sadly for them, to be misclassified by their readers as an LLM is just as bad as using the damn things.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>One of the dirty non-secrets about LLMs is that they were trained on a vast corpus of writing, without consulting the authors of that writing to see if they were cool with it. Individual authors like me can’t do a great deal about it, so are easy to ignore, but music companies aren’t exactly known for taking this kind of thing lying down. So they are <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/business/2026/aug/31/aanthropic-sued-alleged-theft-songs-ai-train-claude">suing over the use song lyrics for LLM training</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Sony Music Publishing and Warner Chappell, music publishers who manage the copyright of songs on behalf of songwriters and composers, are seeking damages for alleged misuse of “tens of thousands” of copyrighted works by Anthropic.
[…]
The plaintiffs claim they are victims of “one of the largest and most blatant ongoing thefts of intellectual property in history”.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Looking at it a broader societal point of view, there is an argument that the benefits of LLMs could be worth far more than any losses to us authors. But we should not forget that these tools are built on a foundation they used without our consent, and that should be taken into account as we regulate these tools and the fruits they provide.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/Steve_Yegge/status/2094947586373505122">Steve Yegge:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>All models, no matter how smart, will eventually build systems that they can no longer understand or maintain, if you let them. Fable 5 finally outbuilt itself, and flailed on me for a week. Fable 5.1 looks like it will fix it. For now. But you have to keep an iron grip on system size, or it’ll run away from you.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was going through some slightly-related work and discovered that the Creating Passionate Users blog had disappeared from the internet (and has been gone since maybe a year ago). For those who don’t know, <strong>Creating Passionate Users</strong> was one of the treasures of the Golden Age of internet blogging. It was the work of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathy_Sierra">Kathy Sierra</a>, also known for co-creating the “Head First” series of computer books. It talked about user experience, and remains some of the best writing on the topic, full of sparkling insights that greatly influenced my thinking, as well as many folks more engaged on user-experience work.</p>

<p>Sadly not just was the blog ahead of time in its content, it was a harbinger of the darker side of the internet, as Kathy came under attack from a particularly virulent form of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/NetNastiness.html">Net Nastiness</a>. That led her to retreat from active participation on the web, and we’ve missed her ever since.</p>

<p>Fortunately the Wayback Machine did its great duty, and <a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20250909191913/https://headrush.typepad.com/creating_passionate_users/">we can still read its snapshot</a>. I’ve often thought that, if I had a clone to spare, I’d like to create a guided tour of Creating Passionate Users to help readers today read that excellent material. (And if you’re reading this Kathy, and want it still hosted on the web, I’d be delighted to.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/simonwillison.net/post/3muwzopsoqs26">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>“I don’t know the answer myself, but I asked a blowhard I know and he took a wild guess, here’s what he said: “</p>

  <p>How I interpret pasted replies from an LLM in online conversations</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jessica Kerr loves the feeling of being part of a team of people that learns from each other and from the codebase they are building as extensions of themselves - she incorporates the term <a href="https://jessitron.com/2018/04/15/the-origins-of-opera-and-the-future-of-programming/">symmathesy</a> for this: a learning system composed of learning parts (both the people and the code).</p>

<p><a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">“But now agents!”</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There was a turning point last year where I noticed that not only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them, at least in conjunction with my own code. They’re more thorough, as well as faster. How am I supposed to be responsible for this system, when I don’t understand each line of code?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She has a habit of digging out old terms and ideas and applying them to our digital world. To frame what’s happening, she digs out two bits of latin</p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>Verum Factum: I made it, so I get it</li>
    <li>Vexationes Artium: Put it to the test [i.e. experiments]</li>
  </ul>
</blockquote>

<p>Agents can’t have Verum Factum knowledge, since it’s gone once their context window clears. They can use Vexationes Artium, running tests to see if something is working.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If we want agents to write working, reliable code for us, we have to double down, 10x down on our objective verification. We need to vexate that code in artful ways. And we have the agent help us with that, with its thoroughness.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This is, of course, true of those building these AI models - they certainly don’t have a Verum Factum knowledge of how they work, all they can do is come up with artful vexations to figure out what might be going on in there.</p>

<p>What does that mean for us humans? Kerr says The Enlightenment elevated the idea that reason was the special quality of mankind. But now we’ve built machines that can reason. We need to focus instead on human qualities that the machines don’t have. Imagination is more important to us now than reason. And the essence of our humanity is in our relationships with other people.</p>

<p>This material was put together for a conference talk, it’s available in <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Nvr0klVxw5c">video</a> and in <a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">text</a> form. Generally I far prefer text, but there’s so many delights in her talk that I found it worth watching. (Such as her current definition of legacy software, as code that wasn’t built with agents in mind - and is thus lacking in testability and consistency.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jim Gumbly outlines the decision space for <a href="https://www.jimgumbley.com/blog/timing-tradeoff-skynet-timeline.html">three people who are making vital judgments</a> about controls and regulations for AI: Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, and David Sacks (for US government).</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Sacks worries that binding oversight could weaken American leadership and the financing that sustains it. Sam and Dario face commercial incentives to keep producing more capable models. The value of those models increasingly comes from persistence, cooperation and permission to act. Those same properties increase the demands on containment and oversight. Some advances also make the evidence harder to interpret.</p>

  <p>Each decision can be understandable on its own. Their combined effect can still move deployment ahead of our ability to control it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A complication for all this is the issue of observability of these models.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>OpenAI reports that Astra is better aligned on its evaluations while also being less monitorable. Its traces can be shorter and less informative. In adversarial tests, where it was instructed to evade oversight, it could conceal some behaviour from monitors.</p>

  <p>[…]</p>

  <p>That matters to the familiar software cycle of releasing something, observing what happens and improving it. The cycle depends on the quality of the observations. Fewer warning flags are reassuring only to the extent that the warning system remains capable of detecting the relevant failures.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There’s an El Niño year coming up, and The Grauniad reports that climate scientists predict <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/environment/2026/sep/03/el-nino-supersizing-before-our-eyes-united-nations?CMP=share_btn_url">this El Niño is going to be a spectacularly hot one.</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The most recent data, from Monday, shows the temperature of the ocean at the heart of El Niño at 2.6C above the 30-year average. That is already close to the highest anomaly ever recorded in the satellite data era, 3.1C in 2015, with months to go before the peak is expected.</p>

  <p>That peak is forecast to reach about 4C in November, according to the average of 14 different models. Data from analysis of corals, tree rings and historical documents suggest no El Niño has reached this level in the last millennium, said Zeke Hausfather, a climate analyst.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>If these forecasts end up being accurate, will this make a difference to how seriously people are taking the climate crisis?</p>

 -->

### [v0.30.0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.30.0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>v0.30.0</h1>
<h2>Highlights</h2>
<p>This release features 762 commits from 315 contributors (104 new)!</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>New models</strong>: DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56214">#56214</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56228">#56228</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56208">#56208</a>) with the whole KV stored in MXFP8 through the FlashMLA V4.1 record on SM100 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56893">#56893</a>), DeepGEMM Mega-mHC (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56962">#56962</a>), and async Engram prefetch with Engram DP sharding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56512">#56512</a>); DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Vision-Exp (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54566">#54566</a>), also on ROCm (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55107">#55107</a>) and with LoRA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55897">#55897</a>); GLM-5.3-Flash (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53906">#53906</a>) with EPLB (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55119">#55119</a>); K2-Horizon (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55063">#55063</a>); Cohere Compass (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54774">#54774</a>); Bailing V3 VL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55921">#55921</a>); Nanbeige4.2 via the Transformers backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56071">#56071</a>); and a DeepSeek-V4 CPU backend with AVX512/AMX sparse MLA, indexer, mHC and compressor kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55355">#55355</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Fast Start</strong>: a persistent per-GPU weight-cache daemon holds post-quantized, TP-sharded weights in GPU memory so restarting engines map them over CUDA IPC with <code>--load-format ipc_cache</code> instead of reloading from disk (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54921">#54921</a>), now covering FP4 checkpoints (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55465">#55465</a>) and multi-node TP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55468">#55468</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Watermarking</strong>: Gumbel-max watermarked generation and detection with a keyed PRF, per-request opt-out and an example detection endpoint (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54053">#54053</a>); dual-key Gumbel-max makes it compatible with speculative decoding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56122">#56122</a>); the Rust frontend forwards the per-request controls (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56338">#56338</a>).</li>
<li><strong>HiSparse</strong>: a host-resident tier for sparse-MLA decode that spills KV pages to pinned host memory under GPU pressure and serves top-k misses from a per-request GPU hot buffer, enabled through <code>HiSparseConnector</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53781">#53781</a>), with Prometheus counters (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56061">#56061</a>), a host cache shared across TP ranks (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56629">#56629</a>), and the attention config inferred from the connector (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57041">#57041</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Model Runner V2</strong>: dual-batch overlap in eager mode (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/50945">#50945</a>) and with FULL CUDA graphs for microbatched steps (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51700">#51700</a>); MTP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/46994">#46994</a>) and EAGLE3/DFlash/DSpark (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/50514">#50514</a>) speculative decoding under pipeline parallelism; adaptive verification for every draft-model speculator through an online acceptance estimator (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/52228">#52228</a>); gc frozen during graph capture, cutting capture from 12s to 2s and engine init from 28.9s to 8.2s on H200 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54646">#54646</a>); <code>--return-sampling-mask</code> compacted on GPU, fixing an about 2x RL step-time regression (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54901">#54901</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Qwen3.8-Flash-Next performance</strong>: separate prefill and decode QSA indexer kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54513">#54513</a>), fused PLE kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54517">#54517</a>), FP8 indexer cache (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54890">#54890</a>), padded-index skipping in sparse GQA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54873">#54873</a>), fused PLE residual and QSA output gate (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55309">#55309</a>), UVA PLE offload and Engram tensor parallelism via <code>--engram-config</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54371">#54371</a>), and torch.compile removed from the NVIDIA implementation so FP8 fits on a single GB300 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55272">#55272</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Kimi K3 performance</strong>: native CUDA AttnRes default on SM100 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54261">#54261</a>), KDA mixed-batch gather/scatter removed (5.2-7.7% E2E throughput, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56159">#56159</a>), grouped FP8 MLA cache insertion (4-6x kernel speedup at small batch, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55356">#55356</a>), DSV3 low-latency GEMM on strided tensors (12-81% kernel speedup, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54565">#54565</a>), overlapped TP8 KDA projections (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54697">#54697</a>), FlashInfer KDA kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55364">#55364</a>), internal prefix checkpoints with partial prefix caching and speculative decoding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53614">#53614</a>), and symmetric DCP disaggregation for hybrid Mamba models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55531">#55531</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Large scale serving</strong>: PCP+DCP on sparse-MLA models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56157">#56157</a>), PCP with single-module MTP and replicated DSpark (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56107">#56107</a>) and decode-only FULL CUDA graphs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53867">#53867</a>), Elastic EP reusing CUDA graphs across reconfiguration (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54985">#54985</a>), an opt-in FlashInfer PCIe IPC all-reduce for NVLink-less boxes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53576">#53576</a>), DeepEP v2 async finalize overlapping shared experts with combine (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/52781">#52781</a>), Mooncake Store heterogeneous TP sharing (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53129">#53129</a>), a KVCR secondary-tier adapter (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53624">#53624</a>), and encoder-cache sharing over NIXL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/47941">#47941</a>) and Mooncake (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/41567">#41567</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Quantization</strong>: targeted online quantization through <code>quantization_config.targets</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51285">#51285</a>) and on partially pre-quantized checkpoints from any quant method (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51392">#51392</a>), W4A16 DSA with the <code>nvfp4_fp8_ds_mla</code> KV cache (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51724">#51724</a>), FlashInfer CuTeDSL NVFP4 W4A16 default over Marlin on SM100/103 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53014">#53014</a>), NVFP4 in the torch linear backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53319">#53319</a>), per-quantization linear backend overrides (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51204">#51204</a>), AutoRound 2/3/5/6/7-bit on CUDA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/52890">#52890</a>), and DeepSelect top-k for the DSA sparse indexer (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56464">#56464</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Breaking changes</strong>: scale-out endpoints are opt-in on plain <code>vllm serve</code> via <code>--enable-scale-out</code>, replacing <code>VLLM_ENABLE_SCALE_OUT_ENDPOINTS</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54579">#54579</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55176">#55176</a>); GPTQ activation ordering (<code>g_idx</code>) removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54809">#54809</a>); items deprecated for 0.29 removed, including the <code>VLLM_PREFIX_CACHE_RETENTION_INTERVAL</code> and <code>VLLM_MM_HASHER_ALGORITHM</code> env vars (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55353">#55353</a>); the <code>all</code> Mamba cache mode deprecated (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55041">#55041</a>); <code>python -m vllm.entrypoints.grpc_server</code> deprecated in favor of <code>vllm serve --grpc</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56746">#56746</a>); YaRN aligned with Transformers so vendor YaRN aliases no longer re-scale <code>max_model_len</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56446">#56446</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h2>Release Artifacts</h2>
<h3>Python Wheels</h3>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Platform</th>
<th>Install</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>PyPI (CUDA 13.0)</td>
<td><code>pip install vllm</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>PyPI (CUDA 13.0, uv)</td>
<td><code>uv pip install vllm --torch-backend=auto</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>ROCm</td>
<td><code>pip install vllm --extra-index-url https://wheels.vllm.ai/rocm/0.30.0/rocm723</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>XPU</td>
<td><code>uv pip install vllm --extra-index-url https://wheels.vllm.ai/0.30.0/xpu --extra-index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/xpu --index-strategy unsafe-best-match</code></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
